=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There is an H \times W grid. Let (h,w) denote the cell at the h-th row from the top and the w-th column from the left. A non-negative integer A_{h,w} is written in cell (h,w).
Takahashi starts at cell (sh,sw) and will perform Q changes to the grid. The i-th change is given by a character d_i (d_i is one of L, R, U, D) and a non-negative integer a_i, meaning Takahashi will do the following:

- Move one cell in the direction d_i. That is, if d_i is L, move left; if R, move right; if U, move up; if D, move down by one cell. Then, let the destination cell be (h,w), and set A_{h,w} to a_i.

It is guaranteed that in each change, he can move one cell in direction d_i.
After each change, print the answer to the following problem:

A sequence of cells P = ((h_1,w_1), \ldots, (h_{M},w_{M})) is said to be a path if and only if it satisfies all of the following conditions:

- (h_1,w_1) = (1,1), (h_{M},w_{M}) = (H,W), and M = H + W - 1.
- For every i with 1 \leq i \leq M-1, either (h_{i+1}, w_{i+1}) = (h_i + 1, w_i) or (h_{i+1}, w_{i+1}) = (h_i, w_i + 1).

There are \binom{H+W-2}{H-1} paths. For a path P = ((h_1,w_1), \ldots, (h_{M},w_{M})), define f(P) = \prod_{1\leq i\leq M}A_{h_i,w_i}. Print the sum, modulo 998244353, of f(P) over all paths P.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
H W
A_{1,1} \cdots A_{1,W}
\vdots
A_{H,1} \cdots A_{H,W}
Q sh sw
d_1 a_1
\vdots
d_Q a_Q

Output

Print Q lines.
The i-th line should contain the sum, modulo 998244353, of f(P) over all paths P after performing the i-th change to the grid.

Constraints


- 2 \leq H, W \leq 200000
- HW \leq 200000
- 0 \leq A_{h,w} < 998244353
- 1 \leq Q \leq 200000
- 1 \leq sh \leq H, 1 \leq sw \leq W
- 0 \leq a_i < 998244353
- H, W, A_{h,w}, Q, sh, sw, and a_i are integers.
- Each d_i is L, R, U, or D.
- In each change, Takahashi can move one cell in the direction d_i.

Sample Input 1

2 3
1 2 3
4 5 6
3 2 2
U 7
R 8
L 9

Sample Output 1

456
666
822


- Initially, Takahashi is at (2,2).
- Move up, then set A_{1,2} to 7. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 3 \times 6=126.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 5 \times 6=210.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.


- Move right, then set A_{1,3} to 8. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 8 \times 6=336.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 7 \times 5 \times 6=210.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.


- Move left, then set A_{1,2} to 9. The value of f(P) for each path is:
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(1,3),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 9 \times 8 \times 6=432.
- P=((1,1),(1,2),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 9 \times 5 \times 6=270.
- P=((1,1),(2,1),(2,2),(2,3)): f(P)=1 \times 4 \times 5 \times 6=120.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's analyze column transformation T(A) more. T(A) is lower triangular with entries suffix products. Product of such transformations across columns: Does it have a compact representation of size O(H)? Maybe yes! Let's compute.

For two columns A then B, matrix C = T(B) T(A). Entry C[h][i] = sum_{k=i}^h (∏_{j=k}^h B_j)(∏_{j=i}^k A_j). This is sum over split k. Can this be expressed as? Let's define prefix products of A and B? Let P_A[i]=∏_{j=1}^i A_j, P_B. Then T_A[h][i]=P_A[h]/P_A[i-1], T_B[h][k]=P_B[h]/P_B[k-1]. Then C[h][i] = P_B[h] * Σ_{k=i}^h 1/(P_B[k-1] P_A[i-1]?) This depends on h and i as product of a function of h times a function of i times sum over k between i and h. Not low-rank generally but maybe can be represented by prefix sums of P_A[k]/P_B[k-1]. Specifically C[h][i] = P_B[h] / P_A[i-1] * (S[h]-S[i-1]) where S[t]=Σ_{k=1}^t P_A[k]/P_B[k-1]. This is O(1) per entry if we know prefix products and S. So the product of two columns has a special "Cauchy" form. Product of many columns? Let's generalize.

For a sequence of columns (or cells along path), the total DP from start to cell (h,w) maybe can be expressed using products? Let's derive closed form for dp[h][w] over grid with arbitrary A. Sum over all monotone paths product. This is like complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial? For a 1D row, dp over cells with down? Maybe can be computed by dynamic programming; no simple O(H) representation for arbitrary W? But maybe column transformation product has a representation of size O(H) using prefix sums recursively. Let's examine product of three columns.

T(A) T(B) T(C)? Actually order: apply C? Let's define T_col for each column. Product over columns from left to right. For two columns, C[h][i] = P_B[h] / P_A[i-1] * (S[h]-S[i-1]). This is a sum of two rank-1 terms? Expand: C[h][i] = P_B[h] S[h] / P_A[i-1] - P_B[h] S[i-1] / P_A[i-1]. First term rank-1 (function h * function i). Second term? P_B[h] * (S[i-1]/P_A[i-1]) also rank-1! Wait S[i-1] depends on i, P_A[i-1] depends on i; yes second term is function h * function i. So C is rank ≤2! Let's check: C[h][i] = U1[h]*V1[i] + U2[h]*V2[i]? Specifically U1[h]=P_B[h] S[h], V1[i]=1/P_A[i-1]; U2[h]=-P_B[h], V2[i]=S[i-1]/P_A[i-1]. Yes rank 2. Interesting! Product of two column transformations is low-rank (rank 2) despite HxH lower triangular. Product of many columns might have rank growing? Let's test.

T(A) itself is rank? T(A)[h][i]=P_A[h]/P_A[i-1] for i<=h, zero for i>h. This is not low-rank because of triangular support, but as a full matrix maybe rank H? Actually T_A = diag(P_A[h]) * L * diag(1/P_A[i-1]) where L is lower triangular all ones. L (ones lower triangular) has full rank H. So T_A rank H. But product of two T's rank ≤2? Wait product of full-rank matrices should be full rank generically. Did we make mistake? Let's test H=2.

A=(a1,a2), T_A = [[a1,0],[a2*a1,a2]].
B=(b1,b2), T_B = [[b1,0],[b2*b1,b2]].
Product C = T_B T_A:
C[1][1]=b1*a1.
C[2][1]=b2*b1*a1 + b2*a2*a1 = b2*a1*(b1+a2).
C[2][2]=b2*a2.
Matrix [[a1 b1, 0], [a1 b2(b1+a2), a2 b2]].
Rank? determinant = a1 b1 * a2 b2 nonzero, rank 2. Our rank-2 expression for H=2 is full rank (rank 2). For H=3, rank ≤2 would imply determinant zero for 3x3, impossible if invertible. So expression must be wrong.

Let's recompute C[h][i] = Σ_{k=i}^h T_B[h][k] T_A[k][i].
T_A[k][i] = ∏_{j=i}^k A_j = P_A[k]/P_A[i-1].
T_B[h][k] = ∏_{j=k}^h B_j = P_B[h]/P_B[k-1].
Then C = P_B[h]/P_A[i-1] * Σ_{k=i}^h P_A[k]/P_B[k-1].
Let S[t]=Σ_{k=1}^t P_A[k]/P_B[k-1].
Then Σ_{k=i}^h = S[h]-S[i-1].
So C[h][i] = P_B[h] S[h] / P_A[i-1] - P_B[h] S[i-1] / P_A[i-1].

- If x is southeast of (r,c) (including? dp changes), the first factor P_x changes.

- If x is both? If x is southeast, Q_x (suffix to end) does not depend on A[r][c] because paths from x to end don't go northwest to (r,c). If x is northwest, P_x doesn't depend. Let's formalize. We maintain actual dp and g for all cells? Initially compute dp0, g0. When A[r][c] changes by δ:
Let P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1] (actual current).
Let Q = g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1] (actual current).
Then Δdp[r][c] = δ * P.
Δg[r][c] = δ * Q.
For any cell (i,j) southeast of (r,c) (i>=r,j>=c), Δdp[i][j] = Δdp[r][c] * R_{rc->ij}, where R_{rc->ij} is sum over paths from (r,c) to (i,j) of product of A along path excluding (r,c). But note this R depends on current A values, which themselves change over time. If we store range updates using the R at the time of update, and later A changes, the propagation of old dp delta through later cells also changes? Wait linearity: The DP recurrence is linear in A values. If A at an intermediate cell changes later, the contribution of an old source to dp at a target should be multiplied by the new A at that intermediate cell? Let's check. Suppose at time 1, A_c changes, creating Δdp at source. At time 2, A_x changes. The total dp at target is nonlinear in A_c and A_x; contributions from source to target depend on product of A along paths, including A_x. If A_x changes later, the old source's contribution to dp at target should be updated too. Thus storing a fixed range update kernel R computed at time of source update is not sufficient; future updates to intermediate cells alter the propagation of old sources. This is the core difficulty. In dynamic linear systems, point updates to coefficients change the Green's function globally. So lazy range updates with fixed kernels fail unless we recompute kernels.

However, the answer S itself is a polynomial; if we maintain dp/g actual, a point update to A affects dp/g at all southeast/northwest, and the effect size depends on current dp/g (which include all previous updates). We have column transformations T(A) of the form y_h = A_h(y_{h-1}+x_h). This is equivalent to:
y_h / (A_1...A_h)? Let's define normalized variables to simplify.

Let P_h = ∏_{j=1}^h A_j (prefix product in column). Define z_h = y_h / P_h. Then recurrence:
y_h = A_h (y_{h-1} + x_h)
Divide by P_h = P_{h-1} A_h:
z_h = y_h / P_h = (y_{h-1} + x_h) / P_{h-1} = z_{h-1} + x_h / P_{h-1}.
So z_h = z_{h-1} + x_h / P_{h-1}. With z_0=0. Thus
z_h = Σ_{i=1}^h x_i / P_{i-1}.
Then y_h = P_h * Σ_{i=1}^h x_i / P_{i-1}.
This matches T.

Now consider applying multiple columns. Let's define for each column w, prefix products P_w[h]=∏_{j=1}^h A[j,w]. Let y_w[h]=dp[h,w]. Define normalized z_w[h] = y_w[h] / P_w[h]. Then z_w[h] = Σ_{i=1}^h y_{w-1}[i] / P_w[i-1]. But y_{w-1}[i] = P_{w-1}[i] * z_{w-1}[i]. So
z_w[h] = Σ_{i=1}^h (P_{w-1}[i] / P_w[i-1]) * z_{w-1}[i].
This is again a lower triangular transformation but with different kernel. Let R_w[i] = P_{w-1}[i] / P_w[i-1]. Then z_w = L * diag(R_w) * z_{w-1}, where L is lower triangular all ones. Not obviously simpler.

Consider reverse process. The grid values change over time. At time t, before update, A values are from last assignment. The DP values dp/g at time t-1. This is not linear, but maybe can be expanded as sum over paths of product over "last update times" of cells. For a segment of columns [l,r], to combine left segment L and right segment R, we need the product matrix M = M_R * M_L. To compute the final answer for the whole grid, we need e_H^T M_total e_1. If each node stores:

- u = e_H^T M_node (backward row vector length H)

For a leaf T_w, applying to vector is O(H) (recurrence). For a segment, applying its matrix to a vector could be done by recursively descending: apply left child to vector, then right child. Let's revisit dynamic update of R and L. Maybe we can update R and L in O(len(k)) using local formulas? Let's derive more carefully.

When A_c changes by δ:
P = R_{k-1}[up] + R_{k-1}[left] (where R_{k-1} is dp on previous diagonal).
Q = L_k[c] (backward coefficient at c, independent of A_c).
ΔR_k[c] = δ P.
For d>k, ΔR_d = T_d ΔR_{d-1}. T_d is sparse. If we want to update all future R_d, cost total affected cells. But maybe we don't need to update R_d for all cells; we only need R at cells that will be updated in the future. Wait at the time after current update, before future updates, A values are current. To know R at future time, future updates may change intermediate A. So not static.

However, if we process offline from the end backwards, maybe future updates are known and can be incorporated. Let's try reverse time.

Let S_t be answer after t updates. We want S_t. The coefficient C_t for update t uses state before update t (after t-1). If we process time backwards, we can maintain some dual values? Suppose we know the final grid after all Q updates. The answer S_Q can be computed. Can we derive S_{t-1} from S_t and update t? The update t changed A[p_t] from old value to new value. If we reverse it (set back to old), we can compute S_{t-1} = S_t - δ_t * C_t, where C_t is coefficient at time t-1. But C_t depends on state before update, not after. Could we compute C_t from state after update? The coefficient C_t is independent of A[p_t], so it is the same before and after update! Wait C_t = (dp_up+dp_left)*(g_down+g_right) at cell p_t. Does it depend on A[p_t]? No, as argued. Therefore C_t is identical before and after update t (provided other cells unchanged). Thus if we process backwards from final state, we can compute C_t using the state after update t (which is the current state in reverse before reverting), then revert A[p_t] to old value and update DP? But we still need to maintain DP state as we revert updates. However maybe we can compute C_t from the final state and the sequence of updates using reverse DP without updating all cells? Let's see.

In reverse, we start with final grid after Q. We need for t=Q down to 1:
- C_t = coefficient at p_t in current grid (after update t, before reverting).
- S_{t-1} = S_t - δ_t * C_t.
- Revert A[p_t] to previous value (the value before update t). This is another point update (negative δ) to the grid. Then continue.

So we still have the same dynamic point update problem, but now we need C_t at the cell before reverting. Maybe we can compute C_t using current dp/g; same issue.

Let's think about the walk property. Takahashi moves one cell each update. The sequence p_t is a walk. In reverse, p_t also a walk. Maybe we can maintain dp/g along the walk incrementally? When moving from p_t to p_{t+1}, the updated cell is adjacent. The coefficient C_{t+1} at p_{t+1} depends on dp/g at neighboring cells. Could we update dp/g locally along the walk in O(1) per step? Let's test.

Suppose we maintain dp and g values for all cells? A point update at p affects many cells, but maybe if we only need coefficients at the next cell p_{t+1}, which is adjacent to p_t, the change in dp/g at p_{t+1}'s neighbors due to update at p_t can be computed locally? Let's examine.

At time t, update at p=(r,c). Next update at p'=(r',c') adjacent (one of L,R,U,D). We need coefficient at p' before update t+1, i.e., after update t. The coefficient at p' uses dp at up/left neighbors of p' and g at down/right neighbors. These cells are near p. Could the update at p affect dp at up/left neighbors of p'? If p' is adjacent to p, then up/left neighbors of p' are within distance 2 of p. The update at p changes dp at cells southeast of p. If p' is southeast of p (R or D), then up/left neighbors of p' might be southeast of p or equal? Let's enumerate.

p=(r,c).
If p' = R: (r,c+1). Up/left neighbors: (r-1,c+1) and (r,c). (r,c) is p itself, dp at p changes due to update. (r-1,c+1) is northeast of p (r-1,c+1): not southeast (row less), so dp unchanged. So P for p' includes dp[r,c] which changed. Need know new dp at p. We can compute Δdp[p] = δ*P_old. So local.
If p' = D: (r+1,c). Up/left neighbors: (r,c) and (r+1,c-1). dp at p changed; other not southeast. Local.
If p' = L: (r,c-1). Up/left neighbors: (r-1,c-1) and (r,c-1). Neither southeast of p? (r,c-1) is west, row same, col less; not southeast. So P for p' unchanged by update at p. But wait p' is west, so paths to p' cannot pass through p (p is east), so dp at p' unchanged. Good.
If p' = U: (r-1,c). Up/left neighbors: (r-2,c) and (r-1,c-1). Neither southeast of p? row less, so unchanged.

Thus the P part of coefficient at next cell changes only if next move is R or D, and then only one of the two neighbors is p (the current cell), whose dp changed. The other neighbor is not affected. So we can update P for next coefficient locally! What about Q = g_down+g_right at p'? Update at p changes g at cells northwest of p. If p' is northwest of p (L or U), then down/right neighbors of p' may include p or cells northwest? Let's check.

If p' = L: (r,c-1). Down/right neighbors: (r+1,c-1) and (r,c). (r,c)=p is east, g at p changed. Other (r+1,c-1) is southwest (row greater, col less) not northwest of p? For g update, A at p affects g at cells northwest of p (i<=r,j<=c). (r+1,c-1) row greater, so not affected. So Q changes by g at p.
If p' = U: (r-1,c). Down/right neighbors: (r,c) and (r-1,c+1). p is down, g at p changed; other northeast not affected.
If p' = R or D (southeast of p), Q unchanged because p' is southeast, its down/right neighbors are further southeast, not northwest of p.

So for the immediate next update, the coefficient C_{t+1} can be updated locally using the change in dp at p (if moving R/D) and change in g at p (if moving L/U). But wait, update at p also changes dp at p, which may affect dp at cells further southeast, including future positions after multiple steps. If we only update local coefficient for next step, what about later steps? The walk may move away and then come back to southeast region; dp at those cells will be stale. But maybe we can maintain dp and g values along the walk by propagating changes as the walk moves? Let's think.

We need coefficient for each update at p_t. The state before update t includes all previous updates. The effect of update at p_{t-1} on coefficient at p_t is local as above. But the effect of earlier updates on p_t may be nonlocal. However, maybe if we maintain dp and g values for all cells along the current walk position and update them incrementally as we move, we can account for all previous updates? We can maintain dp values only for cells that are currently relevant (the walk positions and their neighbors). When a point update occurs at p, it creates a "delta source" that propagates southeast. Instead of updating all affected dp now, we can store the source. However, if we process online, when we need dp at x at time t, all past sources have been created and the A values along paths from source to x are their current values at time t (since updates after source may have changed them). The contribution of a source to dp at x is not simply fixed; it should be recomputed using current A. But maybe we can compute dp at x from scratch using the current grid? That would be expensive.

Maybe maintain dp values for all cells but update only along the walk's "frontier"? Let's simulate a point update at p and then the walk moves. The effect of the update on dp at future cells will be needed only when the walk is at those cells or their neighbors. Let's test with H=2,W=1e5, walk moves right every step. Update at (1,c) affects dp[1][w] for all w>=c and dp[2][w] for all w>=c. Let's formalize. The coefficient at current cell p=(r,c) before update uses:
P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1] (north and west neighbors).
Q = g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1] (south and east neighbors).
These neighbors are adjacent to p. If we maintain dp values for all cells that are northwest of the current position (including current row/col prefix) and g values for all cells southeast of current position, then P and Q are available. When we move one step:
- If move R to (r,c+1): The new cell is east. We need dp[r][c] (west neighbor) and dp[r-1][c+1] (north neighbor). dp[r][c] is known (current cell). dp[r-1][c+1] is north of new cell; is it northwest of old position? Old (r,c), dp[r-1][c+1] has row < r, col > c. It is not northwest of old position (col greater). Did we maintain it? Maybe not. But we can compute dp[r-1][c+1] from dp[r-2][c+1] and dp[r-1][c] if we have column c+1 values above. If we haven't visited column c+1 before, we may need to compute the entire prefix of that column from top to r-1. This can be done by sweeping down the column using known dp values from previous column? To compute dp[i][c+1] for i=1..r-1, we need dp[i-1][c+1] and dp[i][c]. dp[i][c] for i<r are known if we maintained column c. So we can compute the new column prefix up to r-1 in O(r) when moving right. Similarly, if move D, compute row prefix up to c-1 in O(c). If move L or U, we move back to already computed cells, so O(1). This is like maintaining a DP table for the rectangle between start and current position, expanding as the walk moves right/down, and shrinking? If the walk moves left/up, we don't need to compute new cells; but later moving right/down again may require recomputing cells that were computed before but whose northwest values may have changed due to updates at cells in the expanded rectangle? Wait if we move left, we don't update any cells to the east? The current position moves west; updates happen at new cells. If we later move east back to a cell, its dp may have changed due to updates at cells northwest of it that occurred while we were west? But if we moved west, updates at west cells are northwest of the east cell? For an east cell (r,c+1), a west cell (r,c) is west, not northwest? It is same row, smaller col; paths to (r,c+1) can come from left (r,c), so dp at (r,c+1) depends on dp at (r,c). But when we move east, we can recompute dp at (r,c+1) from dp[r][c] (updated) and dp[r-1][c+1]. So we can compute on demand. For cells further east, not needed until visited. Thus maybe we can maintain dp values for cells in a "frontier" and compute new cells on demand using recurrence from known west/north neighbors. This is essentially running DP along the walk's trajectory, not updating the whole grid.

But there is a catch: When we compute dp for a new cell (r,c+1), we need dp[r-1][c+1] (north). If we haven't computed that cell before, we need to compute the entire prefix of column c+1 above r. To compute dp[i][c+1], we need dp[i-1][c+1] and dp[i][c]. dp[i][c] for i<r may be known from previous column if we maintained it. If later we move down from (r,c+1) to (r+1,c+1), we need dp[r][c+1] (known) and dp[r+1][c] (known if column c row r+1 computed? Maybe not). We can compute row r+1 prefix? Let's design a data structure that maintains dp values for all cells in the rectangle [1..r] x [1..c] (the northwest rectangle of current position). When moving right to c+1, we need to extend the rectangle to include column c+1 rows 1..r. We can compute dp[i][c+1] for i=1..r using dp[i][c] (known) and dp[i-1][c+1] (just computed). Cost O(r). When moving down to r+1, extend rectangle to include row r+1 columns 1..c. Cost O(c). When moving left or up, the rectangle shrinks; we can keep computed values for future expansion? If updates occurred while outside the rectangle? Updates are always at current position, which is inside the rectangle? Let's see. If current position is (r,c), the rectangle [1..r]x[1..c] contains all cells northwest of current. When we move left to (r,c-1), rectangle shrinks to [1..r]x[1..c-1]. The update at (r,c-1) is inside the new rectangle. Later moving right to (r,c), rectangle expands back to include column c. But when expanding, we can recompute column c from scratch using current dp in column c-1 and above. Cost O(r). So we can always recompute expanded cells on demand.

Similarly, we need g values for cells southeast of current position. We can maintain a "southeast rectangle" [r..H] x [c..W]? When moving down/right, it shrinks; when moving up/left, it expands and we recompute g values on demand. This is symmetric.

This is a huge insight! Let's develop.

We maintain dp values for all cells in the northwest rectangle of the current position? Actually to compute P for current cell, we need dp at north and west neighbors, which are inside [1..r] x [1..c] (including current? north (r-1,c) row<=r-1,col<=c; west (r,c-1) row<=r,col<=c-1). Similarly, for g, we maintain g for all cells in the southeast rectangle [r..H] x [c..W]? Cells with row<r or col<c are not in the rectangle. The only cell in the rectangle that is northwest of current? Current itself. So g[current] changes. Thus we only need update g[current] locally. This is a linear operator T_h on sequences of length W. It is lower triangular with entries segment products. Point update at A[h][c] changes T_h. But if we compute dp prefix only up to sw, dp[0][1]=1 and dp[0][w>1]=0. For h=1,w=1, dp[1][1]=A11. For h=1,w=2..sw, recurrence works. For h>1, works. We should not compute dp for columns >sw.

Compute dp prefix:
for h in range(1,H+1):
    row_dp = dp[h]; row_A = A[h]
    for w in range(1, sw+1):
        row_dp[w] = row_A[w] * (dp[h-1][w] + row_dp[w-1]) % MOD
But for h=1,w=1, dp[0][1]=1, row_dp[0]=0, gives A11. Good.

Compute g suffix from sw:
for h in range(H,0,-1):
    row_g = g[h]; row_A = A[h]
    for w in range(sw, W+1):
        row_g[w] = row_A[w] * (g[h+1][w] + row_g[w+1]) % MOD
For h=H,w=W, g[H+1][W]=1, row_g[W+1]=0, gives A[H][W]. For h=H,w<W, g[H+1][w]=0, row_g[w+1] known. For h<H,w=W, row_g[W+1]=0, g[h+1][W] known. Good.

Initial ans = dp[H][W]? But dp prefix only up to sw. If sw may be < W, dp[H][W] is not computed. We need total answer S initially. We can compute full dp for all columns once to get ans, or compute g[1][1]? g suffix from sw includes column 1 only if sw=1; not. We need S. We can compute full dp initially O(N) separately, or compute ans using g? Since g suffix from sw doesn't include columns <sw. We can compute full dp for ans, but then we don't want to keep full dp? We can compute full dp in a temporary array or just compute dp for all columns initially and then "forget" columns >sw? But dp for columns >sw would be stale after updates; we can set them to 0 or leave but not use. Computing full dp costs O(N) and is fine. We can compute dp for all columns 1..W initially, store ans=dp[H][W]. Then we need dp prefix up to sw. The full dp values for columns <=sw are correct. For columns >sw, we will recompute on expansion; we can leave them but not rely. However, if we leave them, when expanding right we must overwrite them. That's fine. But if we compute full dp, dp[0][1]=1 base works. So initial ans = dp[H][W]. Similarly, we could compute full g for all columns and then use suffix from sw; but g for columns <sw would be stale after updates; we can leave and overwrite on expansion left. Computing full g costs O(N) too. We can compute full g from W down to 1, store ans? ans = g[1][1]. But we only need g suffix from sw; full g for columns <sw will be overwritten when moving left. That's okay. So simplest: compute full dp and full g initially O(N) each. Then set current position (sh,sw). dp prefix valid for columns <=sw (they are correct initially), g suffix valid for columns >=sw. For columns outside, values are initial but will be recomputed before use after any updates? Wait if we move right, we recompute new column from current prefix, overwriting initial. If we move left, we recompute g column. So stale outside values never used. Good.

But there is a subtlety: When moving right, to compute new column c+1, we use dp[h][c] for all h (valid) and dp[h-1][c+1] (just computed). We do not use old dp[h][c+1] except as storage. So fine. When moving left expanding g, we compute g[h][c-1] using g[h+1][c-1] (just computed) and g[h][c] (valid). We do not use old g[h][c-1]. Fine.

Now, after an update at (nr,nc), we recompute dp vertical segment in column nc and g vertical segment in column nc. This keeps dp/g for column nc up-to-date. What about dp for columns <nc? They are unaffected. g for columns >nc unaffected. Good.

What about dp for columns >nc that were previously computed and maybe stored? They are not valid; we will recompute when moving right. But if we move right, we compute column nc+1 using dp column nc (valid). If we had previously computed column nc+1 and it is stored, we overwrite. Good.

What about g for columns <nc stored? Overwrite when moving left.

Now, answer ans maintenance: Initially ans = full dp[H][W] (or g[1][1]). After each update, ans += delta * P * Q. But does this formula remain valid when we expand dp/g before update? We compute P and Q using dp/g after expansion but before A update. Since expansion just computes dp/g for new prefix/suffix with current A, they are correct for the current grid. The coefficient C for cell (nr,nc) is independent of A[nr][nc], so using old A at destination is fine. Then ans update by delta*C. Then we update A and dp/g. Good.

Let's test with sample manually.

Sample1 H=2,W=3. H<=W, no transpose. Initial A:
[1,2,3;4,5,6], start (2,2). Compute full dp:
dp[1][1]=1, dp[1][2]=2, dp[1][3]=6; dp[2][1]=4, dp[2][2]=5*(2+4)=30, dp[2][3]=6*(30+6)=216. ans=216.
g full:
g[2][3]=6, g[2][2]=5*(6+0)=30, g[2][1]=4*(30+0)=120; g[1][3]=3*(6+0)=18, g[1][2]=2*(30+18)=96, g[1][1]=1*(120+96)=216.

Query1 U: move from (2,2) to (1,2). nc=2 same, no expansion. Current (1,2). P = dp[0][2]+dp[1][1]=0+1=1. Q = g[2][2]+g[1][3]=30+18=48. old A=2, new=7, delta=5. ans=216+5*1*48=456. Update A[1][2]=7. Recompute dp column 2 from row1 to H:
i=1: dp[1][2]=7*(dp[0][2]+dp[1][1])=7*1=7.
i=2: dp[2][2]=5*(dp[1][2]+dp[2][1])=5*(7+4)=55.
Recompute g column 2 from row1 down to1? For nr=1, recompute i=1..1? Wait g affected rows <=nr, so only i=1. g[1][2]=A[1][2]*(g[2][2]+g[1][3])=7*(30+18)=336. But g[2][2] remains 30 (unchanged). Is that correct? Updating A[1][2] affects g at (1,2) only within suffix? g[2][2] is southeast of (1,2), paths from (2,2) to end do not pass through (1,2), so unchanged. Good. dp[2][2] changed to 55, but dp[2][3] (column 3) is not in prefix? Current c=2, dp column 3 is outside prefix and stale. We won't use until moving right. ans=456.

Query2 R: move to (1,3). nc=3 > c=2, expand dp prefix by column 3. Compute dp[h][3] for h=1..H using current A (A[1][3]=3, A[2][3]=6) and dp column 2 updated:
h=1: dp[1][3]=3*(dp[0][3]+dp[1][2])=3*(0+7)=21.
h=2: dp[2][3]=6*(dp[1][3]+dp[2][2])=6*(21+55)=456.
Wait dp[2][3] is ans? Actually ans currently 456. dp[2][3] computed as 456, which equals answer after previous update. Good.
g suffix shrinks (nc>c): discard column 2, g suffix now columns 3..W. g[1][3]=18, g[2][3]=6 valid.
Current (1,3). P = dp[0][3]+dp[1][2]=0+7=7. Q = g[2][3]+g[1][4]=6+0=6. old A[1][3]=3, new=8, delta=5. ans=456+5*7*6=666. Update A[1][3]=8. Recompute dp col3 from row1:
i=1: dp[1][3]=8*(0+7)=56.
i=2: dp[2][3]=6*(56+55)=666.
Recompute g col3 rows<=1: g[1][3]=8*(g[2][3]+0)=8*6=48. ans=666. Good.

Query3 L: move to (1,2). nc=2 < c=3, expand g suffix by column 2 (new suffix columns 2..3). Compute g[h][2] for h=H..1 using current A and g column 3:
h=2: g[2][2]=A[2][2]=5 * (g[3][2]=0 + g[2][3]=6) =30.
h=1: g[1][2]=A[1][2]=7 * (g[2][2]=30 + g[1][3]=48) =7*78=546.
dp prefix shrinks (nc<c): discard column 3, dp prefix columns 1..2 valid (dp[1][2]=7, dp[2][2]=55).
Current (1,2). P = dp[0][2]+dp[1][1]=1. Q = g[2][2]+g[1][3]=30+48=78. old A[1][2]=7, new=9, delta=2. ans=666+2*1*78=822. Update A[1][2]=9. Recompute dp col2:
i=1: dp[1][2]=9*(0+1)=9.
i=2: dp[2][2]=5*(9+4)=65.
Recompute g col2 rows<=1: g[1][2]=9*(30+48)=702. ans=822. Matches sample.

Great! This algorithm seems correct and O(H) per move/update. Need handle transposition and direction mapping.

Let's prove invariants more formally for final explanation (though final only code, but we need be confident).

Invariant at the start of each query (before move):
- Current position (r,c).
- For all h, w with 1<=w<=c, dp[h][w] equals the sum of products over paths from (1,1) to (h,w) in the current grid (A values as of now).
- For all h, w with c<=w<=W, g[h][w] equals the sum of products over paths from (h,w) to (H,W) in the current grid.
- ans = total sum = dp[H][W] = g[1][1] (and also dot?).

When moving:
- If R: c increases. Need dp for new column c+1. Since dp for columns <=c is valid and A unchanged, recurrence computes dp for column c+1 for all rows. This extends invariant to c+1. g for columns >=c was valid; after moving, suffix should start at c+1. The old column c is no longer needed; we can consider it invalid. The values for columns >=c+1 remain valid because A unchanged and g recurrence doesn't depend on column c for cells in columns >=c+1? Wait g[h][w] for w>=c+1 depends on g[h][w+1] and g[h+1][w], not on column c. So they remain valid. Good.
- If L: c decreases. g for new column c-1 computed using g for column c (valid) and A unchanged; extends suffix to c-1. dp for columns <=c-1 was valid; column c no longer needed. Good.
- If U/D: c unchanged, both valid.

Then update at destination:
- P and Q computed from valid dp/g.
- ans update.
- A changes. For dp, only cells (h,c) with h>=r can change because any cell with w<c is not reachable from (r,c) (would need left move), and cells with w>c are not in prefix. Within column c, recurrence propagates downward. Recomputing from r to H restores validity.
- For g, only cells (h,c) with h<=r can change because cells with w>c are southeast, and cells with w<c not in suffix. Within column c, recurrence propagates upward. Recomputing from r to 1 restores validity.
Thus invariant holds after update.

Complexities:
- Initial full dp/g O(H*W)=O(N) <=2e5.
- Each query: move expansion at most one column, O(H). Update recompute dp O(H-r+1) and g O(r), total O(H). H<=sqrt(N)<=447 (after transpose H<=W, H*W<=2e5, so H<=sqrt(2e5) ~447). Q<=2e5, total ~90M loops. Python should be okay if optimized.

Need optimize implementation for Python.

Implementation details:

Input parsing:
- Read all ints via sys.stdin.buffer.read().split().
- H,W first.
- Then H*W A values.
- Then Q, sh, sw.
- Then Q lines: d_i a_i. d_i is bytes/str. We'll parse tokens.

Transposition:
If H > W, we want H <= W. Let's transpose grid. Original H0,W0. New H=W0, new W=H0. A_new[new_h][new_w] = A_old[old_h= new_w][old_w= new_h]? Let's derive: new coordinates (h',w') = (old_w, old_h). So A_new[h'][w'] = A_old[w'][h'].
Start: new sh = old sw, new sw = old sh.
Directions mapping:
Old L: old col -1 -> new row -1 => U.
Old R: old col +1 -> new row +1 => D.
Old U: old row -1 -> new col -1 => L.
Old D: old row +1 -> new col +1 => R.
So map: L->U, R->D, U->L, D->R.
We'll implement if transposed.

But wait, if we transpose, the monotone path from (1,1) to (H,W) in original corresponds to path in transposed? Original path moves down (row+1) or right (col+1). In transposed, old down => new right? old row+1 => new col+1, yes right. old right => new row+1, down. So yes.

Now, data structures with 1-indexing.

We need A as list of lists. If H<=447 and W up to 2e5, A rows length W+2. Building A:
A = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
If not transposed:
for h in 1..H:
  for w in 1..W:
    A[h][w] = next int
If transposed:
Read old values into flat list or old A? We can fill A_new directly while reading old row-major. Old A_old[h][w] for h=1..H0,w=1..W0. New A[new_h=w][new_w=h] = old value. Since input row-major, we can fill A[w][h] = val. Need initialize A with dimensions (W0+2) x (H0+2). We'll know H0,W0. So:
if H0 <= W0:
  H=H0; W=W0; A = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
  for h in 1..H:
    for w in 1..W: A[h][w]=val
  sh=sh0; sw=sw0; trans=False
else:
  H=W0; W=H0; A = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
  for h in 1..H0:
    for w in 1..W0:
      val=...
      A[w][h]=val
  sh=sw0; sw=sh0; trans=True

Need read Q and start after A. Input format: H W, then A lines, then Q sh sw, then Q pairs. We'll parse all tokens. Since H*W <=2e5, tokens ~4e5, okay.

Parsing directions:
tokens list of bytes. After reading A and Q,sh,sw, we have Q pairs: d_i token (bytes) and a_i int. We'll process in loop. If transposed, map direction.

Now, dp/g initialization:
dp = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
g = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
dp[0][1] = 1
g[H+1][W] = 1

Compute full dp:
for h in range(1,H+1):
    Arow = A[h]
    drow = dp[h]
    dprev = dp[h-1]
    # for w in 1..W:
    for w in range(1,W+1):
        drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w-1]) % MOD
This is O(N). But drow[w-1] is current row previous column; for w=1, drow[0]=0. Good.
Compute full g:
for h in range(H,0,-1):
    Arow = A[h]
    grow = g[h]
    gnext = g[h+1]
    for w in range(W,0,-1):
        grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w+1]) % MOD
For w=W, grow[W+1]=0; gnext[W] for h=H uses g[H+1][W]=1. Good.

ans = dp[H][W] % MOD (should equal g[1][1]).

We computed full dp/g. Now we need ensure dp for columns >sw and g for columns <sw are considered invalid. We will overwrite when expanding. But if we later move right, we compute column c+1 using dp[h-1][c+1] which might currently hold old initial value. In our expansion code, we must compute from top to bottom and overwrite each dp[h][c+1] before using it as dp[h-1][c+1]. For h=1, dp[0][c+1] is outside row 0, which is 0 (except dp[0][1]=1? Wait dp[0][c+1] for c+1>1 is 0; we never set dp[0][w>1]=1. Good). For h>1, dp[h-1][c+1] has just been computed in previous iteration. So old value not used. Good.
Similarly for g expansion left: compute from bottom to top, g[h+1][c-1] just computed; old not used.

Now, move and update logic in loop.

We need current position r,c (1-indexed). At start of each query, before processing, we have current position from previous update (or initial). We'll read d and a.

Direction mapping if transposed:
dir_map = {ord('L'):'U', ord('R'):'D', ord('U'):'L', ord('D'):'R'}? Tokens are bytes like b'U'. We can compare bytes directly.

In loop:
d = tokens[idx]; a = int(tokens[idx+1]); idx += 2
if transposed:
    if d == b'L': d = b'U'
    elif d == b'R': d = b'D'
    elif d == b'U': d = b'L'
    elif d == b'D': d = b'R'

Move:
if d == b'R':
    # expand dp prefix by column c+1
    nc = c + 1
    # compute dp[1..H][nc]
    # Need A[h][nc], dp[h-1][nc], dp[h][nc-1]
    for h in range(1,H+1):
        dp[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h-1][nc] + dp[h][nc-1]) % MOD
    # g suffix now starts at nc; old column c invalid. No need to modify g.
    c = nc
    # r unchanged
elif d == b'L':
    nc = c - 1
    # expand g suffix by column nc
    for h in range(H,0,-1):
        g[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h+1][nc] + g[h][nc+1]) % MOD
    c = nc
    # r unchanged
elif d == b'U':
    r -= 1
    # c unchanged
elif d == b'D':
    r += 1
    # c unchanged

Wait for R, we also need to update current column c to nc. For L, update c. For U/D, update r. The destination is (r,c) after move.

But note: For R, we expand dp before updating c. The dp expansion uses dp[h][nc-1] which is old column c, valid. Good. For L, expand g before updating c. Good.

Now, after move, current position (r,c). We need compute P and Q:
P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1]
Q = g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1]
But wait, dp[r][c-1] is in prefix (since c-1 < c) and valid. dp[r-1][c] valid. g[r][c+1] in suffix (c+1 > c) valid. g[r+1][c] valid. Good.

If r=1, dp[0][c] = 0 (except dp[0][1]=1? Wait dp[0][1]=1 is used for dp[1][1] base. But for P at cell (1,c), dp[0][c] should be 0 for c>1, and for c=1, dp[0][1]=1? Let's check coefficient at (1,1) if ever updated. P = dp[0][1]+dp[1][0] = 1+0 =1. Is that correct? For start cell, paths from start to (1,1) excluding (1,1) is empty product =1. Yes. But dp[0][1]=1 is artificial and should be used only for P at (1,1). For P at (1,c>1), dp[0][c]=0. Our dp[0] row has dp[0][1]=1, dp[0][w>1]=0. So dp[r-1][c] works: if r=1,c=1, gives 1; if r=1,c>1, gives 0. Good.
For Q at (H,W), g[H+1][W]=1 artificial. If updating (H,W), Q = g[H+1][W]+g[H][W+1] =1+0=1, correct (empty suffix product). If updating (H,c<W), g[H+1][c]=0, good. If updating (r,W), g[r][W+1]=0. Good.

Then old_val = A[r][c]; delta = a - old_val; if delta negative, mod.
ans = (ans + delta * P % MOD * Q) % MOD. In Python, do ans = (ans + delta * P * Q) % MOD; delta may be negative; Python % handles. But for speed, compute delta = (a - old_val) % MOD, then ans = (ans + delta * P % MOD * Q) % MOD. Since delta modulo MOD, works because all modulo. If delta negative, modulo gives positive equivalent.

Then A[r][c] = a.

Recompute dp column c from r to H:
# dp[r][c] = A[r][c] * (dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1])
for h in range(r, H+1):
    dp[h][c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dp[h][c-1]) % MOD
Note: For h=r, dp[r-1][c] valid; dp[r][c-1] valid. For h>r, dp[h-1][c] just updated; dp[h][c-1] valid. Good.

Recompute g column c from r down to 1:
for h in range(r, 0, -1):
    g[h][c] = A[h][c] * (g[h+1][c] + g[h][c+1]) % MOD
For h=r, g[r+1][c] valid; g[r][c+1] valid. For h<r, g[h+1][c] just updated; g[h][c+1] valid. Good.

Then output ans. We need collect outputs in list and join.

Potential issue: When moving R, we expand dp prefix by column c+1. But if c+1 > W? Guaranteed move valid, so not. When moving L, c-1 >=1. Good.

Potential issue: We computed full dp/g initially. When we move R, we expand dp prefix but do not invalidate g column c? g suffix should now start at c+1. The old g column c is no longer valid for future? If we later move L back, we will recompute g column c (old) using g column c+1. So we don't need to clear. But what about g values in column c after an update at (r,c) before moving R? They were updated. After moving R, if we later move L, we recompute g column c from scratch using current A and g column c+1. This will overwrite the old g column c, so fine. But wait, when we moved R, the update at destination (r,c+1) occurred after expansion. The g suffix after move starts at c+1, so g column c is not maintained. If we later move L, we need g column c for the new current position. We recompute it using g column c+1 (valid) and A column c. This recomputation from scratch will correctly include all updates, including the update at (r,c+1)? g[h][c] depends on g[h][c+1] and g[h+1][c]. g[h][c+1] is valid and includes updates in columns >=c+1. So yes.

Similarly, when moving L, dp column c+1 is no longer maintained; if later move R, recompute from dp column c.

Now, ans maintenance after moving R/L before update: We expand dp/g with old A. But ans remains total S for current grid. Is total S equal to dp[H][c] for any c? Wait dp[H][c] is sum of paths from start to (H,c), not to (H,W), so not total unless c=W. Our ans is maintained separately. We don't use dp[H][c] as ans except initially. The invariant for dp prefix is for cells up to column c, not total answer. The total answer S is maintained by update formula. Does ans remain correct after expansions? Expansions don't change A, so S unchanged. We don't modify ans during expansion. Good.

But after an update, we recompute dp column c and g column c. Does ans update formula plus recomputation keep ans consistent with dp/g? Yes.

Let's test another scenario: Update at a cell that is not in the current prefix? It is always destination after move, and we expand prefix/suffix to include it before update. Good.

Now, complexity of initial full dp/g: O(N). But if H<=W, H<=447, W up to 2e5. Full dp loops H*W=N=2e5, fine. Full g same. The move expansion loops H per R/L, update loops up to H for dp and up to H for g. So per query O(H) ~ <=447. Q=2e5 => ~90M iterations. Each iteration does modular multiplication and list accesses. In Python, 90M may be near limit but likely okay if optimized (PyPy maybe 2-3 sec? Actually 90M Python loops ~5-8 sec). Need optimize.

Can we reduce constant? H max 447, Q max 2e5 => 89.4M for move expansions + updates. Each update does two loops totaling H+1 iterations. Move R/L does H iterations. Worst-case all moves R/L, so per query: move expansion H + update dp (H-r+1) + update g (r) ≈ 2H+1 ~ 895 iterations. 2e5*895=179M iterations. That might be high but maybe still okay in PyPy if simple? Need optimize further.

We can combine update dp/g loops? They are separate. But H=447, 179M modular operations could be ~10-15s. Need see if acceptable. We can optimize by using local variables, avoiding modulo if possible? Values <MOD, product <MOD^2 ~1e18, Python int okay. We need modulo each assignment. Could use if val>=MOD? Multiplication of two mod numbers up to 1e9, sum up to 2e9, product up to 2e18, modulo needed. Python % is costly. 180M % operations too high.

Maybe we can reduce loops by noting update dp/g only need recompute if A changed. Yes every query changes A, so loops needed. But we can optimize using list references and while loops? For loops in Python are okay. 180M might be too high.

Can we choose orientation to minimize H? We already H<=W, H<=447. If original H=447,W=447, H=447. Q=2e5, worst 180M. Maybe still okay in PyPy with optimized code? AtCoder Python time maybe 2 sec? Need better.

Maybe we can reduce per query to O(H) but with smaller constant: move expansion only for R/L, not U/D. Worst-case walk can be all R, so per query expansion H + update loops H = 2H. 2*447*2e5=178.8M. If all U/D, no expansion, update loops H = 89M. So worst 180M.

Can we avoid recomputing full vertical segment for dp and g? When A at (r,c) changes, dp column c from r to H changes. But maybe we can update ans and only recompute dp/g at the current cell? Future updates in the same column below/above may need dp/g at those cells. If we don't recompute, they become stale. However, if the walk later moves down/up in the same column, we might need them. We could recompute lazily when needed. But the walk can oscillate vertically, causing repeated recomputation. Recomputing full segment each update is O(H). Maybe acceptable.

Can we reduce update loops by recomputing only from r to H for dp and 1 to r for g; sum = H+1, not 2H. Wait dp loop length H-r+1, g loop length r, total H+1. Move expansion loop H for R/L. So per R update total 2H+1. If all R, 179M. If all U/D, total H+1 ~448 per query, 89M. So worst 180M.

Maybe use C-optimized operations? Not.

Could we maintain dp and g in flat lists and use slice operations? No, recurrence sequential.

Maybe choose H to be the smaller dimension, but if original H=W=447, H=447. If original H=2,W=100000, H=2, per query ~5 loops, very fast. Worst H=447 occurs when grid is square 447x447, N=200k. In that case W=447 too, so W small. The walk can move R/L at most 447 columns, but Q=2e5 can oscillate. H=447. 180M loops. Could we instead choose orientation with H=447; no alternative.

Let's see if we can reduce move expansion cost. For R, we compute dp for new column c+1 all H rows. But we only need dp values in that column for future updates. dp = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
dp[0][1] = 1
for h in range(1, H+1):
    Arow = A[h]
    drow = dp[h]
    dprev = dp[h-1]
    # local variables

        drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w-1]) % MOD
For g:

g[H+1][W] = 1
for h in range(H, 0, -1):
    Arow = A[h]
    grow = g[h]
    gnext = g[h+1]
    for w in range(W, 0, -1):
        grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w+1]) % MOD
ans = dp[H][W]

Now, in query loop, we need update current r,c. We can use local variables for speed. But A, dp, g are lists; we'll assign.

Pseudo:
out = []
r, c = sh, sw
for _ in range(Q):
    d = next(it)
    a = int(next(it))
    if transposed:
        ...
    if d == b'R':
        nc = c + 1
        # expand dp
        for h in range(1, H+1):
            dp[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h-1][nc] + dp[h][c]) % MOD
        c = nc
    elif d == b'L':
        nc = c - 1
        for h in range(H, 0, -1):
            g[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h+1][nc] + g[h][c]) % MOD
        c = nc
    elif d == b'U':
        r -= 1
    else: # D
        r += 1
    # compute P,Q
    P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1]
    Q = g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1]
    old = A[r][c]
    delta = (a - old) % MOD
    ans = (ans + delta * P % MOD * Q) % MOD
    A[r][c] = a
    # update dp column c from r to H
    for h in range(r, H+1):
        dp[h][c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dp[h][c-1]) % MOD
    # update g column c from r down to 1
    for h in range(r, 0, -1):
        g[h][c] = A[h][c] * (g[h+1][c] + g[h][c+1]) % MOD
    out.append(str(ans))

In update dp loop, we use `dp[h][c-1]`; c is current column. If c=1, dp[h][0]=0. Good. In update g loop, `g[h][c+1]`; if c=W, g[h][W+1]=0. Good.

In L expansion, compute g for new column c-1. If c-1 = W? L from c=W+1 impossible. Good.

Let's write optimized loops:

MOD = 998244353
H1 = H + 1
W1 = W + 1

# dp full
for h in range(1, H1):
    Arow = A[h]
    drow = dp[h]
    dprev = dp[h-1]
    for w in range(1, W1):
        drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w-1]) % MOD

# g full
for h in range(H, 0, -1):
    Arow = A[h]
    grow = g[h]
    gnext = g[h+1]
    for w in range(W, 0, -1):
        grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w+1]) % MOD

In query loop, to avoid repeated global lookups, assign `mod = MOD`, `A_local=A`, etc. But Python local variables already.

Expansion R:
nc = c + 1
for h in range(1, H1):
    dp[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h-1][nc] + dp[h][c]) % mod
c = nc

Expansion L:
nc = c - 1
for h in range(H, 0, -1):
    g[h][nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h+1][nc] + g[h][c]) % mod
c = nc

for h in range(r, H1):
    dp[h][c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dp[h][c-1]) % mod
Update g:
for h in range(r, 0, -1):
    g[h][c] = A[h][c] * (g[h+1][c] + g[h][c+1]) % mod

For update dp:

    dp[h][c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dp[h][c-1]) % mod
This accesses dp[h] twice. Could do:

    dph = dp[h]
    dph[c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dph[c-1]) % mod
Similarly g:
for h in range(r - 1, 0, -1):
    ghh = g[h]
    ghh[c] = A[h][c] * (g[h+1][c] + ghh[c+1]) % mod
Expansion R:
for h in range(1, H1):
    dph = dp[h]
    dph[nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h-1][nc] + dph[c]) % mod
Expansion L:
for h in range(H, 0, -1):
    ghh = g[h]
    ghh[nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h+1][nc] + ghh[c]) % mod
This reduces list lookups. We'll use.

Initial full dp:
for h in range(1, H1):
    Arow = A[h]
    drow = dp[h]
    dprev = dp[h-1]
    for w in range(1, W1):
        drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w-1]) % mod
Initial full g:
for h in range(H, 0, -1):
    Arow = A[h]
    grow = g[h]
    gnext = g[h+1]
    for w in range(W, 0, -1):
        grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w+1]) % mod

Before final, let's maybe write a small proof in mind to ensure no hidden flaw.

The key invariant: dp prefix columns <=c, g suffix columns >=c. When moving R, we compute new dp column using recurrence from left column and top. This is valid because dp for new column depends only on left column (same row) and top cell (same column). We compute top-down, so top cell available. It does not depend on any cells to the right. Thus correct. When moving L, compute new g column using recurrence from right column and bottom. g[h][c-1] depends on g[h][c] (right) and g[h+1][c-1] (bottom). Compute bottom-up, so bottom available. Correct.

When updating A[r][c], within dp prefix, only cells in column c with row >=r change. Why not cells in columns >c? They are not in prefix. Why not cells in column c with row <r? Paths to those cells cannot pass through (r,c) because (r,c) is southeast. So unchanged. The recurrence propagates downward. Similarly g. Thus invariant.

The answer update formula: total S = sum over paths product. For a fixed cell (r,c), S as a function of A[r][c] is linear: S = const + A[r][c] * C, where C = sum over paths through (r,c) of product of other cells. C = (sum of products from start to up/left) * (sum of products from down/right to end) = P*Q. P and Q are independent of A[r][c]. Therefore changing A by delta changes S by delta*P*Q. We compute P,Q from dp/g before update. Good.

Now, one subtlety: P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1] is sum over paths from start to up/left including those cells. The product of other cells on a path through (r,c) is indeed product of prefix path (ending at up/left) times product of suffix path (starting at down/right). The suffix sum from down/right to end including down/right is g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1]. Yes Q. Good.

Now, let's think about if the artificial base dp[0][1]=1 affects P for cell (1,1) as empty prefix. Good.

Now, let's consider if the answer ans after initial full dp is dp[H][W]. But if we later move and expand dp prefix, dp[H][c] for c<W is not total. We don't use it. Good.

Now, let's think about if the update formula for ans should be applied before or after recomputing dp/g? We apply before. The recomputation doesn't change ans. Good.

Now, let's consider if the answer ans could also be updated by expansion? No, expansion doesn't change A. Good.

Now, let's think about if the current position after move should be updated before expansion? We use old c for expansion, then update c. For U/D, r updated. Good.

Now, let's consider if in R expansion, we compute dp for new column using A values that may include the destination cell old value. Yes, because update hasn't happened. Good.

Now, let's consider if in L expansion, compute g for new column using A values including destination old value. Yes.

Now, let's think about if after moving R, the g suffix should start at new c, but we didn't invalidate g column old c. If later we move L, we recompute g column old c. But what if we move L and the destination is old c, and we compute P using dp[r][c-1] (which is old c-1) and dp[r-1][c] (old c). dp old c is valid? After moving R, dp prefix includes old c and new c. After update at new c, we recomputed dp new c. dp old c remains valid because update at new c does not affect dp old c (new c is east of old c, paths to old c cannot pass through new c). So when moving L back to old c, dp old c is valid. Good. We don't need to recompute dp old c on L. We only expand g. Good.

Similarly, after moving L, g old c remains valid? When moving L from c to c-1, update at c-1 does not affect g old c (c is east of c-1? Actually g at old c (east) paths to end go right/down, cannot pass through c-1 (west), so unaffected). So when moving R back, g old c valid. Good.

Now, let's think about if after moving R, dp new column computed before update at new c. Then update at new c recomputes dp new column from row r down. What about dp new column rows <r? They are unaffected by update at (r,new c), so remain as computed. Good.

Now, let's consider if after moving L, g new column computed before update at new c. Then update at new c recomputes g new column from row r up. Rows >r unaffected. Good.

Now, let's think about if the walk moves R and then D. After R update at (r,c+1), dp column c+1 all rows valid. Move D to (r+1,c+1), no expansion. Update at (r+1,c+1) recomputes dp column c+1 from r+1 down and g from r+1 up. This is correct. The dp at (r+1,c+1) before update was computed during R expansion and includes old A at (r+1,c+1). Good.

Now, let's consider if the walk moves R and then U. Similar.

Now, let's think about if the walk moves D and then R. D update at (r+1,c) recomputes dp column c from r+1 down and g column c from r+1 up. Then R expands dp column c+1 using dp column c (updated) and A column c+1. Good.

Now, let's consider if the walk moves D and then L. D update at (r+1,c) recomputes g column c from r+1 up. Then L expands g column c-1 using g column c (updated) and A column c-1. Good.

Since ans is total answer, dp[H][W] should equal ans. Let's check: If current position before move is (r,W-1), dp prefix up to W-1, g suffix from W-1. ans maintained. Moving R expands dp column W. dp[H][W] computed as sum over all paths to (H,W) = total answer. It should equal ans. Our ans is maintained independently; if there is a bug, this could be a check. Let's test sample after query2 current c=3=W, no expansion. After query1 ans=456, dp[2][3] was stale (initial 216) because not in prefix. If we moved R to W, we would recompute and it would match ans. Good.

Now, let's consider if the update loops should use `if delta:` to skip. We'll do:
if a != old:
    delta = (a - old) % mod
    deltaP = delta * P % mod
    ans = (ans + deltaP * Q) % mod
    A[r][c] = a
    dp[r][c] = (dp[r][c] + deltaP) % mod
    for h in range(r + 1, H1):
        dph = dp[h]
        dph[c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h - 1][c] + dph[c - 1]) % mod
    deltaQ = delta * Q % mod
    g[r][c] = (g[r][c] + deltaQ) % mod
    for h in range(r - 1, 0, -1):
        ghh = g[h]
        ghh[c] = A[h][c] * (g[h + 1][c] + ghh[c + 1]) % mod
else:
    # no change, but A already same; no need set
    pass
But if a == old, we don't set A (same). Good.

Now, let's write code:

```
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)
    H0 = int(next(it)); W0 = int(next(it))
    if H0 <= W0:
        H, W = H0, W0
        A = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
        for h in range(1, H+1):
            row = A[h]
            for w in range(1, W+1):
                row[w] = int(next(it))
        trans = False
    else:
        H, W = W0, H0
        A = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
        for h in range(1, H0+1):
            for w in range(1, W0+1):
                A[w][h] = int(next(it))
        trans = True
    Q = int(next(it)); sh = int(next(it)); sw = int(next(it))
    if trans:
        sh, sw = sw, sh
    mod = 998244353
    H1 = H + 1
    W1 = W + 1
    dp = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
    g = [[0]*(W+2) for _ in range(H+2)]
    dp[0][1] = 1
    g[H+1][W] = 1
    for h in range(1, H1):
        Arow = A[h]; drow = dp[h]; dprev = dp[h-1]
        for w in range(1, W1):
            drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w-1]) % mod
    for h in range(H, 0, -1):
        Arow = A[h]; grow = g[h]; gnext = g[h+1]
        for w in range(W, 0, -1):
            grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w+1]) % mod
    ans = dp[H][W]
    r, c = sh, sw
    out = []
    for _ in range(Q):
        d = next(it)
        a = int(next(it))
        if trans:
            if d == b'L':
                d = b'U'
            elif d == b'R':
                d = b'D'
            elif d == b'U':
                d = b'L'
            else:
                d = b'R'
        if d == b'R':
            nc = c + 1
            for h in range(1, H1):
                dph = dp[h]
                dph[nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h-1][nc] + dph[c]) % mod
            c = nc
        elif d == b'L':
            nc = c - 1
            for h in range(H, 0, -1):
                ghh = g[h]
                ghh[nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h+1][nc] + ghh[c]) % mod
            c = nc
        elif d == b'U':
            r -= 1
        else:  # D
            r += 1
        P = dp[r-1][c] + dp[r][c-1]
        Q = g[r+1][c] + g[r][c+1]
        old = A[r][c]
        if a != old:
            delta = (a - old) % mod
            deltaP = delta * P % mod
            ans = (ans + deltaP * Q) % mod
            A[r][c] = a
            dp[r][c] = (dp[r][c] + deltaP) % mod
            for h in range(r + 1, H1):
                dph = dp[h]
                dph[c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h-1][c] + dph[c-1]) % mod
            deltaQ = delta * Q % mod
            g[r][c] = (g[r][c] + deltaQ) % mod
            for h in range(r - 1, 0, -1):
                ghh = g[h]
                ghh[c] = A[h][c] * (g[h+1][c] + ghh[c+1]) % mod
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Now, let's consider if the code's `p_coef` and `q_coef` are not modified by expansion? Expansion computes dp/g but not p_coef/q_coef. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `ans` update should be before or after `A[r][c] = a`; before. We do ans before setting A. Good.

Now, let's consider if the code's `A[r][c] = a` should happen before `dp[r][c]` direct set; yes.

Now, let's think about if the code's `dp[r][c]` direct set should happen before `g[r][c]` direct set; no dependency. We do dp then g.

Now, let's consider if the code's `deltaQ` computed after dp propagation; q_coef still available. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code's `g[r][c]` direct set should happen before g propagation; yes.

Now, let's consider if the code's `for h in range(r - 1, 0, -1):` uses
</think>

=== ANSWER ===
import sys

def main():
    data = sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()
    it = iter(data)

    H0 = int(next(it))
    W0 = int(next(it))

    if H0 <= W0:
        H, W = H0, W0
        A = [[0] * (W + 2) for _ in range(H + 2)]
        for h in range(1, H + 1):
            row = A[h]
            for w in range(1, W + 1):
                row[w] = int(next(it))
        trans = False
    else:
        H, W = W0, H0
        A = [[0] * (W + 2) for _ in range(H + 2)]
        for h in range(1, H0 + 1):
            for w in range(1, W0 + 1):
                A[w][h] = int(next(it))
        trans = True

    num_queries = int(next(it))
    sh = int(next(it))
    sw = int(next(it))

    if trans:
        sh, sw = sw, sh

    MOD = 998244353
    mod = MOD
    H1 = H + 1
    W1 = W + 1

    dp = [[0] * (W + 2) for _ in range(H + 2)]
    g = [[0] * (W + 2) for _ in range(H + 2)]

    # artificial boundaries
    dp[0][1] = 1
    g[H + 1][W] = 1

    # initial full DP
    for h in range(1, H1):
        Arow = A[h]
        drow = dp[h]
        dprev = dp[h - 1]
        for w in range(1, W1):
            drow[w] = Arow[w] * (dprev[w] + drow[w - 1]) % mod

    for h in range(H, 0, -1):
        Arow = A[h]
        grow = g[h]
        gnext = g[h + 1]
        for w in range(W, 0, -1):
            grow[w] = Arow[w] * (gnext[w] + grow[w + 1]) % mod

    ans = dp[H][W]
    r, c = sh, sw
    out = []

    for _ in range(num_queries):
        d = next(it)
        a = int(next(it))

        if trans:
            if d == b'L':
                d = b'U'
            elif d == b'R':
                d = b'D'
            elif d == b'U':
                d = b'L'
            else:
                d = b'R'

        if d == b'R':
            nc = c + 1
            for h in range(1, H1):
                dph = dp[h]
                dph[nc] = A[h][nc] * (dp[h - 1][nc] + dph[c]) % mod
            c = nc
        elif d == b'L':
            nc = c - 1
            for h in range(H, 0, -1):
                ghh = g[h]
                ghh[nc] = A[h][nc] * (g[h + 1][nc] + ghh[c]) % mod
            c = nc
        elif d == b'U':
            r -= 1
        else:  # D
            r += 1

        p_coef = dp[r - 1][c] + dp[r][c - 1]
        q_coef = g[r + 1][c] + g[r][c + 1]

        old = A[r][c]
        if a != old:
            delta = (a - old) % mod
            deltaP = delta * p_coef % mod
            ans = (ans + deltaP * q_coef) % mod

            A[r][c] = a

            dp[r][c] = (dp[r][c] + deltaP) % mod
            for h in range(r + 1, H1):
                dph = dp[h]
                dph[c] = A[h][c] * (dp[h - 1][c] + dph[c - 1]) % mod

            deltaQ = delta * q_coef % mod
            g[r][c] = (g[r][c] + deltaQ) % mod
            for h in range(r - 1, 0, -1):
                ghh = g[h]
                ghh[c] = A[h][c] * (g[h + 1][c] + ghh[c + 1]) % mod

        out.append(str(ans))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
